\hat{Y}(\nu,U_{\nu},V)=\frac{n(K_{\nu})V(V+1)}{d_{3}(K_{\nu})V^{3}+d_{2}(K_{\nu})V^{2}+d_{1}(K_{\nu})V+d_{0}(K_{\nu})},

(3.14)

where
n,d_{0},d_{1},d_{2}
and
d_{3}
are explicit polynomials.

From the proof of Proposition
3.5
, we know that the only pole of
\hat{Y}(\nu,U_{\nu},V)
occurs for
V>1
, so that we can restrict our attention to values of
V
which cancels the derivative of (
3.14
) with respect to
V
. There are 4 values of
V
which cancel
\frac{\partial}{\partial V}\hat{Y}(\nu,U_{\nu},Y)
, they correspond to the roots (in
V
) of the two following polynomials:

\begin{cases}P_{1}&=(K_{\nu}^{2}-3)V^{2}+4(1+K_{\nu})^{2}V+K_{\nu}^{2}-3\\
P_{2}&=(K_{\nu}^{2}-3)V^{2}+(-2K_{\nu}^{2}-8K_{\nu}-10)V+K_{\nu}^{2}-3.\end{cases}

We can check by direct inspection that for
K\in[K_{\nu_{c}},K_{\infty})
, these four roots are all real numbers. We denote
V_{1,1}(K_{\nu})\leqslant V_{1,2}(K_{\nu})
(respectively
V_{2,1}(K_{\nu})\leqslant V_{2,2}(K_{\nu})
) the roots of
P_{1}
(respectively of
P_{2}
). A basic analysis yields

V_{2,1}(K_{\nu})\leqslant V_{2,2}(K_{\nu})<0<V_{1,1}(K_{\nu})\leqslant 1\leqslant V_{1,2}(K_{\nu}).

(3.15)

So that, with the notation of Definition
3.3
,
V^{-}(\nu,U_{\nu})=V_{2,2}(K_{\nu})
and
V^{+}(\nu,U_{\nu})=V_{1,1}(K_{\nu})
.
We can check that, for
\nu>\nu_{c}
, we have
y^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu}):=Y(\nu,U_{\nu},V_{1,1}(K_{\nu}))>2
and that
y^{-}(\nu,t_{\nu}):=Y(\nu,U_{\nu},V_{2,2}(K_{\nu}))<-Y(\nu,U_{\nu},V_{1,1}(K_{\nu}))
.

We then compute the singular expansion of
V
around
y_{\nu}=y_{+}(\nu,t_{\nu})
. There exist functions
\aleph^{V}_{i}(\nu)
for
i\in\{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2}\}
, which do not vanish for
\nu\in(\nu_{c},\infty)
, such that:

V(\nu,U_{\nu},y)=V_{+}(\nu,U_{\nu})+\sum_{i\in\{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2}\}}\aleph^{V}_{i}(\nu)\left(1-\frac{y}{y_{\nu}}\right)^{i}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{y}{y_{\nu}}\right)^{3/2}\right),

(3.16)

see the Maple file
[
1
]
for their explicit expression in terms of
K_{\nu}
.
∎

We now establish the expansion in
t
of the series
V
when
y
is fixed:

Lemma 3.9
.

Fix
y
in
\bar{D}(0,y_{+}(\nu,t_{\nu}))
, then
t^{3}\mapsto V(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),y)
admits the following singular expansion at
t_{\nu}^{3}
:

V(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),y)=\begin{cases}V_{\nu}(y)+\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2}\}}\beth_{i}^{V}(\nu,y)\cdot\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{i}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{3/2}\right)&\text{ for $\nu\neq\nu_{c}$, }\\
V_{\nu_{c}}(y)+\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\{\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3},1,\frac{4}{3}\}}\beth_{i}^{V}(\nu_{c},y)\cdot\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu_{c}}^{3}}\right)^{i}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu_{c}}^{3}}\right)^{4/3}\right)&\text{ for $\nu=\nu_{c}$},\end{cases}

(3.17)

where
V_{\nu}(y):=V(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}),y)
, and
\beth_{i}^{V}(\nu,y)
are explicit functions of
\nu
and
y
, which do not vanish.

Proof.

Fix
\nu>0
and
y\in\bar{D}(0,y_{+}(\nu,t_{\nu}))
. In the algebraic equation (
3.1
) between
y
,
U
and
V
, we replace
U
by its development obtained in Lemma
2.3
to get the singular behavior of
V
. We have to proceed by a case-by-case analysis since the singular expansions for
U
are different for
\nu<\nu_{c}
,
\nu=\nu_{c}
and
\nu>\nu_{c}
. Again all computations are performed in the Maple worksheet
[
1
]
.

Case
\nu<\nu_{c}
.

Recall (
2.8
), which expresses
\nu
as a function of
U_{\nu}=U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3})
. The singular behavior of
U
for
t^{3}
around
t_{\nu}^{3}
obtained in Lemma
2.3
is of the form

U(\nu,t^{3})=U_{\nu}+\sum_{i\in\{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2}\}}\beth_{i}^{U}(\nu)\cdot\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{i}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{3/2}\right),

where
\beth_{1/2}^{U},\beth_{1}^{U}
and
\beth_{3/2}^{U}
are explicit functions of
U_{\nu}
which do not cancel in this range of values for
\nu
.

Equation (
3.1
) gives an algebraic equation between
\nu
,
U=U(\nu,t^{3})
,
V=V(\nu,U,y)
and
y
. Since
y
is fixed, we could plug the expansion of
U
in this equation and obtain the development in
t^{3}
of
V
with
y
as a parameter. However, expressions turn out to be nicer if we replace
y
by its value in terms of
V_{\nu}(y):=V(\nu,U_{\nu},y)
.
The identity

y=\hat{Y}(\nu,U_{\nu},V_{\nu})=\hat{Y}\left(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),V\big(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),y\big)\right),

which is valid for any fixed
t\in[0,t_{\nu}^{3}]
, gives an algebraic equation between
\nu
,
U_{\nu}
,
V_{\nu}
,
U(\nu,t^{3})
and
V\big(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),y\big)
. We can again eliminate
\nu
from this equation by replacing it by its value in terms of
U_{\nu}
. We then replace
U(\nu,t^{3})
by its singular expansion to obtain the following asymptotic behavior for
V\big(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),y\big)
:

V\big(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),y\big)=V_{\nu}+\sum_{i\in\{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2}\}}\beth_{i}^{V}(\nu,y)\cdot\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{i}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{3/2}\right),

(3.18)

where
\beth_{i}^{V}(\nu,y)
are non-vanishing explicit functions of
\nu
and
y
. More precisely, the functions
\beth_{i}^{V}(\nu,y)
we obtain are explicit functions of
U_{\nu}
and
V_{\nu}(y)
. See the Maple companion file
[
1
]
for details.

Case
\nu=\nu_{c}
.

The strategy is similar, but since
\nu
is fixed, computations are slightly less heavy. We refer again to the Maple worksheet.
Notice, that the expansion in
t^{3}
of
U(\nu_{c},t^{3})
obtained in Lemma
2.3
is in
\Big(1-(\frac{t}{t_{\nu_{c}}})^{3}\Big)^{1/3}
rather than in
\Big(1-(\frac{t}{t_{\nu_{c}}})^{3}\Big)^{1/2}
as above. By performing, the exact same line of arguments, we then obtain the desired singular development for
V(\nu_{c},t,ty)
.

Case
\nu>\nu_{c}
.

Computations are slightly more elaborate in this case since
\nu
cannot be expressed as a rational function of
U_{\nu}
. As in the proof of Proposition
3.10
, we rely on the rational parametrization for
U_{\nu}
and
\nu
by
K_{\nu}
given in (
2.10
) and (
2.9
). Apart from this, the strategy of proof is totally similar and we refer to the Maple worksheet for the details of the computation. In particular the asymptotic development for
U(\nu,t^{3})
around
t_{\nu}^{3}
of Lemma
2.3
has the form

U(\nu,t^{3})=U_{\nu}+\sum_{i\in\{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2}\}}\beth_{i}^{U}(\nu)\cdot\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{i}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{3/2}\right),

where
U_{\nu}
,
\beth_{1/2}^{U},\beth_{1}^{U}
and
\beth_{3/2}^{U}
are explicit functions of
K_{\nu}
, defined for
\nu\in(\nu_{c},\infty)
and which do not cancel on this interval, leading to the development of
V(\nu,U(\nu,t),y)
.
∎

3.3
Singular expansions for the generating series of triangulations with monochromatic boundary

In this section, we study the singularities of
Q^{+}(\nu,t,ty)
. As was done with
V
, we first consider the case when
t
is fixed at
t_{\nu}
and study the singularities in
y
of
Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)
. Then, we fix
y
and study the singularities in
t
of
Q^{+}(\nu,t,ty)
.

Proposition 3.10
.

For
\nu>0
, the series
Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)
has radius of convergence
y_{\nu}=y_{+}(\nu,t_{\nu})
and a single dominant singularity at
y_{\nu}
, where it admits the following expansion.

•

For
\nu<\nu_{c}
:

Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)=Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y_{\nu})+\aleph^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\left(1-\frac{y}{y_{\nu}}\right)^{a(\nu)-1}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{y}{y_{\nu}}\right)^{a(\nu)-1}\right);

(3.19)

•

For
\nu\geqslant\nu_{c}
:

Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)=Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y_{\nu})+\aleph_{1}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\left(1-\frac{y}{y_{\nu}}\right)\\
+\aleph^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\left(1-\frac{y}{y_{\nu}}\right)^{a(\nu)-1}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{y}{y_{\nu}}\right)^{a(\nu)-1}\right);

(3.20)

where
\aleph_{1}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)
and
\aleph^{Q^{+}}(\nu)
are explicit non-vanishing functions of
\nu
and

a(\nu)=\begin{cases}5/3&\text{for $\nu<\nu_{c}$,}\\
7/3&\text{for $\nu=\nu_{c}$,}\\
5/2&\text{for $\nu>\nu_{c}$.}\end{cases}

(3.21)

Proof.

The fact that
y_{\nu}
is the unique dominant singularity of
Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)
follows from Proposition
3.7
. It remains to establish the asymptotic behavior at
y_{\nu}
. We rely on the rational parametrization obtained in Theorem
3.1
. From (
3.2
), we see that as long as
V\neq-1
, the asymptotic behavior of
Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)
is driven by the asymptotic behavior of
V
as a function of
y
. It hence suffices to plug in (
3.2
) the singular expansion of
y\mapsto V\Big(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}),y\Big)
obtained in Lemma
3.8
to get the desired results. All computations are available in the Maple companion file
[
1
]
.
∎

A direct application of the transfer theorem (see Theorem
B.1
in Appendix
B.2
) then implies:

Corollary 3.11
.

For any
\nu>0
, we have:

Q^{+}_{\ell}(\nu,t_{\nu})\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}\frac{\aleph^{Q^{+}}(\nu)}{\Gamma\Big(1-a(\nu)\Big)}\cdot\Big(t_{\nu}y_{\nu}\Big)^{-\ell}\ell^{-a(\nu)}.

(3.22)

We now turn to the singular behavior in
t^{3}
of
Q_{+}(\nu,t,ty)
when
y
is fixed.

Proposition 3.12
.

Fix
\nu>0
and
y\in(0,y_{\nu})
, then
Q_{+}(\nu,t,ty)
seen as a series in
t^{3}
has radius of convergence
t_{\nu}^{3}
where it has the following asymptotic expansion:

Q^{+}(\nu,t,ty)=Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)+\beth_{1}^{Q^{+}}(\nu,y)\left(1-\Big(\frac{t}{t_{\nu}}\Big)^{\!3}\right)\\
+\beth^{Q^{+}}(\nu,y)\left(1-\Big(\frac{t}{t_{\nu}}\Big)^{\!3}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}+o\left(\left(1-\Big(\frac{t}{t_{\nu}}\Big)^{\!3}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}\right),

(3.23)

where we recall from Proposition
2.4
that:

\gamma(\nu)=\begin{cases}5/2&\text{for $\nu\neq\nu_{c}$,}\\
7/3&\text{for $\nu=\nu_{c}$.}\end{cases}

Furthermore, for every fixed
\nu>0
, the two coefficients
\beth_{1}^{Q^{+}}
and
\beth^{Q^{+}}
are power series in
y
with radius of convergence
y_{\nu}
and their expression is given by an explicit rational function in
V(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}),y)
.

Remark 3.13
.

For every
p>0
, the series
t^{p}Q^{+}_{p}(\nu,t)
has a unique dominant singularity at
t_{\nu}^{3}
, so that it is reasonable to think that
t_{\nu}^{3}
is also the unique dominant singularity of
Q_{+}(\nu,t,ty)
. This could be rigorously proved by a similar approach as in the proof of Proposition
4.14
. However, we did not prove this statement as it is not needed for our work.

Proof.

For any fixed
\nu>0
and any fixed
y\in(0,y_{\nu})
, the function
Q_{+}(\nu,t,ty)
seen as a series in
t^{3}
has non negative coefficients. It is also clear from its definition that its radius of convergence cannot be larger that
t_{\nu}^{3}
. From Proposition
3.10
, we deduce that its radius of convergence is indeed
t_{\nu}^{3}
. By Pringsheim’s theorem,
Q_{+}(\nu,t,ty)
is then singular at
t_{\nu}^{3}
. It hence remains to prove that the asymptotic behavior at this singularity is the one given in the proposition.

To get the asymptotic expansion of
Q^{+}(\nu,t,ty)
around
t_{\nu}^{3}
, we once again rely on the rational parametrization obtained in Theorem
3.1
. In (
3.2
), we substitute
U
and
V
by their singular expansion obtained respectively in Lemma
2.3
and Lemma
3.9

to get the desired result.

All computations are performed in
[
1
]
.
∎

We will need later the asymptotic development of
\beth^{Q^{+}}
in the sub-critical and critical regime:

Proposition 3.14
.

Fix
\nu\in(0,\nu_{c}]
. Then
\beth^{Q^{+}}(\nu,y)
, seen as a power series in
y
, has a unique dominant singularity at
y_{\nu}=2
, where it has the following singular expansion:

\beth^{Q^{+}}(\nu,y)=\sum_{i\in\left\{\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{3}\right\}}\frac{\aleph_{i}^{\beth^{Q^{+}}}(\nu)}{(2-y)^{i}}+\mathcal{O}(1),

with non vanishing coefficients
\aleph_{i}^{\beth^{Q^{+}}}(\nu)
that are explicit functions of
U_{\nu}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the expression of
\beth^{Q^{+}}(\nu,y)
as a rational function of
V(\nu,U_{\nu},y)
. The development is obtained as usual by replacing
V
by its singular development obtained in Lemma
3.8
. See the Maple companion file
[
1
]
for details.
∎

3.4
Singularities in
y
of
Q^{+}(\nu,t,ty)

In this section, we study the value of the singularities (in
y
) of
Q^{+}(\nu,t,ty)
. Recall the definition of
y_{-}(\nu,t)
and
y_{+}(\nu,t)
given in Definition
3.3
. The next statement studies the singular behavior in
t
of these functions:

Proposition 3.15
.

Fix
\nu>0
. The functions
y_{+}(\nu,t)
and
y_{-}(\nu,t)
of
Q_{+}(\nu,t,ty)
defined in Definition
3.3
are power series in
t^{3}
and have a unique dominant singularity at
t_{\nu}^{3}
. In addition, they have the following asymptotic expansions:

y_{-}(\nu,t)=y_{-}(\nu,t_{\nu})+\\
\beth^{-}_{1}(\nu)\left(1-\Big(\frac{t}{t_{\nu}}\Big)^{\!3}\right)+\beth^{-}(\nu)\left(1-\Big(\frac{t}{t_{\nu}}\Big)^{\!3}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}+o\left(\left(1-\Big(\frac{t}{t_{\nu}}\Big)^{\!3}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}\right),

(3.24)

and

y_{+}(\nu,t)=y_{\nu}+\beth^{+}(\nu)\left(1-\Big(\frac{t}{t_{\nu}}\Big)^{\!3}\right)^{\gamma_{+}(\nu)-1}+o\left(\left(1-\Big(\frac{t}{t_{\nu}}\Big)^{\!3}\right)^{\gamma_{+}(\nu)-1}\right),

(3.25)

where
\beth^{-}_{1}(\nu)
,
\beth^{-}(\nu)
and
\beth^{+}(\nu)
are explicit non-vanishing functions of
\nu
. Furthermore,
\gamma(\nu)=5/2
for
\nu\neq\nu_{c}
or
7/3
for
\nu=\nu_{c}
as defined in Proposition
2.4
, and

\gamma_{+}(\nu)=\begin{cases}7/4&\text{for $\nu<\nu_{c}$;}\\
3/2&\text{for $\nu=\nu_{c}$;}\\
3/2&\text{for $\nu>\nu_{c}$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Recall from Definition
3.3
that
y_{\pm}(\nu,t)
are given respectively by

\hat{Y}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),V_{\pm}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}))),

where
V_{\pm}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3})))
denote the largest negative root and the smallest positive root of the polynomial (
3.6
). This already proves that
y_{\pm}(\nu,t)
are formal power series in
U(\nu,t^{3})
, and therefore in
t^{3}
. To prove that they both have the same singularities as
U(\nu,t^{3})
, and therefore a unique singularity at
t_{\nu}^{3}
, we look at the discriminant in
V
of the polynomial (
3.6
). This discriminant factorizes as:

\frac{1024(-1+2U)(U\nu+U-\nu)(3U^{2}\nu+3U^{2}-3U\nu-3U+\nu)(15U^{2}\nu+15U^{2}-24U\nu-6U+8\nu)^{2}}{(U\nu+U-2)^{4}U^{4}}.

The factors of degree 1 in
U
are clearly not 0 for
|U|\leqslant U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3})
. The first factor of degree 2 in
U
is the polynomial giving
U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3})
for
\nu\leqslant\nu_{c}
and can only be
0
at this value for
|U|\leqslant U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3})
. Finally we can check that the last factor can only be
0
for values of
U
with a modulus larger than
U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3})
(see the Maple companion file
[
1
]
for details). Hence,
y_{\pm}(\nu,t)
have unique dominant singularities at
t_{\nu}^{3}
.

It remains to prove that the asymptotic expansions at
t_{\nu}^{3}
have the form given in the proposition. The calculations share similarities with the proof of Proposition
3.12
. Informally, in the equation (
3.6
) verified by
V_{\pm}
, we can replace
U(\nu,t^{3})
by its singular behavior around
t_{\nu}^{3}
obtained in Lemma
2.3
. It then suffices to identify the right branch for
V
, to get a singular expansion for
V_{\pm}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}))
. Plugging back the expansions for
U(\nu,t^{3})
and
V_{\pm}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}))
in
\hat{Y}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),V_{\pm}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3})))
then yields the desired result.

We now give some details about the computations for the different values of
\nu
. All the computations are performed in the Maple companion file
[
1
]
.

Case
\nu<\nu_{c}
.

From Proposition
3.10
, we know that, for
\nu\leqslant\nu_{c}
, the radius of convergence in
y
of
Q(t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)
is equal to 2, which corresponds to
V_{+}(\nu,U(t_{\nu}^{3}))=1
.

We can indeed check that, after plugging the expansion of
U(\nu,t^{3})
in (
3.6
), one (and only one) of the solutions has a constant term equal to 1, and we can hence compute its expansion around 1. We obtain:

V_{+}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}))=1+\sum_{i\in\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4}\}}\beth_{i}^{V_{+}}(\nu)\cdot\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{i}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{3/4}\right),

(3.26)

where
\beth_{\frac{1}{4}}^{V_{+}}
,
\beth_{\frac{1}{2}}^{V_{+}}
and
\beth_{\frac{3}{4}}^{V_{+}}
are explicit functions of
\nu
. Replacing
U
and
V_{+}
by their expansion in
\hat{Y}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),V_{+}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3})))
gives the desired expansion for
y_{+}(\nu,t)
.

We now move to the singular expansion of the negative singularity
y_{-}(\nu,t)
. From the proof of Proposition
3.10
, we know that
y_{-}(\nu,t_{\nu})
corresponds to
V_{-}(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}))=-2+\sqrt{3}
. We can hence perform the same type of computation around
V=-2+\sqrt{3}
rather than
V=1
to get the desired result.

Case
\nu=\nu_{c}
.

In this case, the value of
y_{+}(\nu_{c},t_{\nu_{c}})
also corresponds to
V_{+}(\nu_{c},U(\nu_{c},t^{3}_{\nu_{c}}))=1
.
This time, when we compute the expansion of
V
, we obtain a different singular behavior, namely:

V_{+}(\nu_{c},U(\nu_{c},t^{3}))=1+\sum_{i\in\{\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{2}\}}\beth_{i}^{V_{+}}(\nu)\cdot\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{i}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{1/2}\right),

(3.27)

where
\beth_{\frac{1}{6}}^{V_{+}}
,
\beth_{\frac{1}{3}}^{V_{+}}
and
\beth_{\frac{1}{2}}^{V_{+}}
are explicit functions of
\nu
. Replacing
U
and
V_{+}
by their expansion in
\hat{Y}(\nu_{c},U(\nu_{c},t^{3}),V_{+}(\nu_{c},U(\nu_{c},t^{3})))
gives the desired expansion for
y_{+}(\nu_{c},t)
.

The expansion around
V_{-}(\nu_{c},U(\nu_{c},t_{\nu_{c}}^{3}))=-2+\sqrt{3}
does not yield any additional difficulties and gives the desired singular expansion.

Case
\nu>\nu_{c}
.

As in the proofs of Proposition
3.10
and of Proposition
3.12
, we parametrize
\nu
and
U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3})
by
K_{\nu}
with (
2.9
) and (
2.10
). The values of
V_{\pm}(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}))
can be calculated explicitly from (
3.15
) as roots of polynomials of degree 2. We obtain from this the two following asymptotic expansions

\displaystyle V_{+}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}))

\displaystyle=V_{+}(\nu,U_{\nu})+\sum_{i\in\{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2}\}}\beth_{i}^{V_{+}}(\nu)\cdot\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{i}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{3/2}\right)

\displaystyle V_{-}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}))

\displaystyle=V_{-}(\nu,U_{\nu})+\sum_{i\in\{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2}\}}\beth_{i}^{V_{-}}(\nu)\cdot\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{i}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{t^{3}}{t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{3/2}\right),

where
\beth_{\frac{1}{2}}^{V_{+}}
,
\beth_{\frac{1}{2}}^{V_{-}}
,
\beth_{1}^{V_{+}}
,
\beth_{1}^{V_{-}}
,
\beth_{\frac{3}{2}}^{V_{+}}
and
\beth_{\frac{3}{2}}^{V_{-}}
are explicit functions of
\nu
.

Replacing
U
and
V_{\pm}
by their expansions in
\hat{Y}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),V_{\pm}(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3})))
gives the desired expansions for
y_{\pm}(\nu,t)
without additional difficulties, after noticing that the term of order
\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)^{1/2}
vanishes in
y_{-}(\nu,t)
but not in
y_{+}(\nu,t)
.
∎

3.5
Monochromatic simple boundary

Recall that
Z^{+}(\nu,t,x)
denotes the generating series of Ising-weighted triangulations with a
simple
monochromatic boundary. It was proved in
[
2
]
that for every
\nu>0
, the series
Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},x)
is well-defined as
t_{\nu}
is the radius of convergence of
Z^{+}_{p}(t)
for every
p>0
and
Z^{+}_{p}(t_{\nu})<\infty
. We start this section with a rational parametrization for
Z^{+}
:

Theorem 3.2
.

Recall the definition of
U
given in Proposition
2.1
. We define
W\equiv W(\nu,U,x)
as the unique formal power series in
\mathbb{Q}[\nu,U]\llbracket x\rrbracket
having constant term
0
and satisfying the following equation:

\displaystyle x=\hat{X}(\nu,U,W),

(3.28)

where
\hat{X}(\nu,U,W)
is the following rational fraction:

\begin{split}\hat{X}(\nu,U,W)&=\frac{8\nu\left(1-2U\right)\left(1-\nu\right)}{8\left(\nu+1\right)^{2}U^{3}-\left(11\nu+13\right)\left(\nu+1\right)U^{2}+2\left(\nu+3\right)\left(2\nu+1\right)U-4\nu}\frac{W}{\left(W+1\right)^{2}}\\
&\qquad\left(W^{2}+\frac{2\left(5\left(\nu+1\right)U^{2}-2\left(3\nu+2\right)U+2\nu\right)}{U\left(U\left(\nu+1\right)-2\right)}W\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\left.-\frac{8\left(\nu+1\right)^{2}U^{3}-\left(11\nu+13\right)\left(\nu+1\right)U^{2}+2\left(\nu+3\right)\left(2\nu+1\right)U-4\nu}{U\left(U\left(\nu+1\right)-2\right)\left(1-\nu\right)}\right).\end{split}

The series
Z^{+}(\nu,t,tx)
is algebraic and admits the following rational parametrization in terms of
U
and
W
:

\displaystyle Z^{+}(\nu,t,tx)=\hat{Q}^{+}\Big(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),W\big(\nu,U(\nu,t^{3}),x\big)\Big)-1,

(3.29)

where
\hat{Q}^{+}(\nu,U,W)
is the rational parametrization for
Q^{+}
given in Theorem
3.1
.

Proof.

As mentioned earlier, the algebraicity of
Z^{+}
is established in
[
2
]
, see in particular Theorem 2.11 in this reference. We only have to check that the expressions given in the statement are solution of the algebraic equation satisfied by
Z^{+}
and the theorem follows by uniqueness of the solution in a similar fashion as Theorem
3.1
. Computations are performed in the Maple file
[
1
]
.
∎

With the rational parametrization of
Z^{+}
the study of the singular behavior of
Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}\,x)
is very similar to the study of
Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)
performed in Proposition
3.10
. We gather in the next statement the information we need on
Z^{+}
.

Lemma 3.16
.

For
\nu>0
, the series
Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}x)
has radius of convergence
x_{\nu}>0
and a single dominant singularity at
x_{\nu}
, where it admits the following expansion:

Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}x)=Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}x_{\nu})+\aleph_{1}^{Z^{+}}(\nu)\left(1-\frac{x}{x_{\nu}}\right)\\
+\aleph^{Z^{+}}(\nu)\left(1-\frac{x}{x_{\nu}}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}+o\left(\left(1-\frac{x}{x_{\nu}}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}\right),

(3.30)

where
\aleph_{1}^{Z^{+}}(\nu)
and
\aleph^{Z^{+}}(\nu)
are explicit functions of
\nu
, which do not vanish, and
\gamma
is the function of
\nu
, which already appeared in the asymptotic developments of
Q^{+}
(Proposition
3.12
) and is given by:

\gamma(\nu)=\begin{cases}5/2&\text{for $\nu\neq\nu_{c}$,}\\
7/3&\text{for $\nu=\nu_{c}$.}\end{cases}

Furthermore, we have

\begin{cases}\dfrac{-\aleph_{1}^{Z^{+}}(\nu)}{1+Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}x_{\nu})}=1&\text{if $\nu<\nu_{c}$,}\\
\dfrac{-\aleph_{1}^{Z^{+}}(\nu)}{1+Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}x_{\nu})}\leqslant\dfrac{\sqrt{7}}{1+\sqrt{7}}&\text{if $\nu\geqslant\nu_{c}$,}\end{cases}

with equality in the last case if and only if
\nu=\nu_{c}
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to what was done with
Q^{+}
and we only point out the differences. To study the singularities of
x\mapsto Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}\,x)
, we can first study the singularities of
x\mapsto W(\nu,U_{\nu},x)
, where we recall that
U_{\nu}=U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3})
. Again we treat separately the case
\nu\leqslant\nu_{c}
and
\nu>\nu_{c}
. Detailed computations are available in the Maple worksheet
[
1
]
.

Case
\nu\leqslant\nu_{c}
.

In this regime,
\hat{X}(\nu,U_{\nu},W)
has a pole at
W=-1
and its stationary points are given by the roots of the polynomial

\left(W-1\right)\left((6U^{2}-6U+1)W^{2}+4(6U^{2}-6U+1)W+6U^{2}-10U+3\right).

(3.31)

We check that for
U\in(0,U_{c}]
, the roots of the polynomial of degree 2 are never in
(-1,1)
and therefore
x\mapsto W(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}),x)
is bijective from
(-\infty,x_{\nu}]
onto
(-1,1]
, where

{}x_{\nu}=\hat{X}(\nu,U_{\nu},1)>0

is the radius of convergence of
x\mapsto Z^{+}(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}),x)
by composition.

Before computing the expansion of
Z^{+}(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}),x)
at
x_{\nu}
, we still have to check that it has no other dominant singularity. The only candidates are the images
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
of the two roots of the polynomial of degree two (
3.31
) by the function
\hat{X}
. We compute explicitly the asymptotic expansions of
Z^{+}
at these two values and see that if
|x_{i}|=x_{\nu}
, the development is non singular (we identify the right branch using the value of
Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}x_{i})
, which has to be of modulus smaller than
Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}x_{\nu}
)).

Now that we know that
x\mapsto Z^{+}(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}),x)
has a unique dominant singularity at
x_{\nu}
, we compute the expansion of
x\mapsto W(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}),x)
at this value by singular inversion and plug it in
\hat{Z}^{+}
to obtain the expansions given in the statement. The coefficients
\aleph_{1}^{Z^{+}}(\nu)
,
\aleph^{Z^{+}}(\nu)
and
Z^{+}(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}),x_{\nu})
are all explicit functions of
U_{\nu}
and establishing the last statement is an easy calculation.

Case
\nu>\nu_{c}
.

As done several times before, we work in this regime by replacing
\nu
and
U_{\nu}
by their value in terms of
K_{\nu}
given in equations (
2.9
) and (
2.10
). The stationary points of
\hat{X}(\nu,U_{\nu},W)
are given by the roots of the polynomial

\left(K_{\nu}^{2}W-K_{\nu}^{2}-8K_{\nu}-3W-13\right)\left(K_{\nu}^{2}W^{2}+4K_{\nu}^{2}W+K_{\nu}^{2}+8K_{\nu}W-3W^{2}+4W-3\right).

We check that the root of the factor of degree
1
is real and smaller that
-1
, and that the factor of degree two has two real roots, one in
(0,1)
that we will denote by
W_{\nu}
, and one in
(1,\infty)
. By global inversion, we see that
x\mapsto W(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}),x)
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus[x_{\nu},\infty)
, where

x_{\nu}=\hat{X}(\nu,U_{\nu},W_{\nu})>0

is the radius of convergence of
x\mapsto Z^{+}(\nu,U(\nu,t_{\nu}^{3}),x)
by composition. The rest is relatively straightforward: we compute the expansion of
\hat{X}
at
W_{\nu}
and plug it into
\hat{Z}^{+}
to get the statement.
∎

4
Boltzmann maps and BDG functions

To study the root spin cluster, we will interpret it in the context of Boltzmann planar maps associated to a given weight sequence, which has been studied intensively in the literature
[
60
,
57
,
23
,
55
,
58
,
49
]
. In this section, we first review the necessary material about this model of maps. This includes universal expressions for their partition functions, and the connection with a pair of bivariate functions
f^{\bullet}
and
f^{\diamond}
, given by the Bouttier–Di Francesco–Guitter bijection.

In a second part, we prove that the root spin cluster has the distribution of a Boltzmann planar map with an explicit weight sequence. We study the asymptotic properties of this weight sequence as well as the singular behavior of the two functions
f^{\bullet}
and
f^{\diamond}
associated to it.

4.1
Background on Boltzmann maps

4.1.1
Partition functions

Fix any sequence of non-negative real numbers
\mathbf{q}=(q_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
such that
q_{k}\neq 0
for some odd
k\geqslant 3
.
Recall that
\mathcal{M}
(respectively
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
) denotes the set of finite non-atomic rooted (respectively and pointed) planar maps. For any
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}
, the
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann weight of
\mathfrak{m}
is defined by

w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathfrak{m})=\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}.

(4.1)

The partition function
w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M})
and the pointed partition function
w_{\mathbf{q}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
of
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann maps are then defined by:

\displaystyle w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}}\,\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)},

\displaystyle w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}}\,\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}=\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}}\,|V(\mathfrak{m})|\,\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}.

Definition 4.1
.

A weight sequence
\mathbf{q}
is said to be
admissible
if its partition function satisfies

w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M})<\infty,

which is equivalent to
w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})<\infty
, see
[
10
, Proposition 4.1]
.

We can associate to any admissible weight sequence
\mathbf{q}
a probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{\mathbf{q}}
on
\mathcal{M}
defined by,

\forall\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M},\quad\mathbb{P}^{\mathbf{q}}\left(\{\mathfrak{m}\}\right)=\frac{w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathfrak{m})}{w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M})}.

(4.2)

A random planar map sampled from
\mathbb{P}^{\mathbf{q}}
is called a
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann map
and is denoted by
\mathbf{M}^{\mathbf{q}}
.

For admissible weight sequences, other important quantities are the so-called disk partition functions and their pointed versions. For
l\geqslant 1
, let
\mathcal{M}_{l}
denote the set of finite rooted planar maps whose root face
f_{r}
is of degree
l
. For every
l\geqslant 1
, the disk function and the pointed disk function are respectively defined by:

\displaystyle W^{(l)}_{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{l}}\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})\setminus\{f_{r}\}}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)},

(4.3)

\displaystyle W^{(l)}_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{l}}|V(\mathfrak{m})|\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})\setminus\{f_{r}\}}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)},

(4.4)

which are all finite if
\mathbf{q}
is admissible.
Note that the case
l=2
is of special interest since it allows to recover the partition functions of the
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann maps by the identities

W^{(2)}_{\mathbf{q}}-1=w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M})\quad\text{and}\quad W^{(2)}_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}-1=w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}),

(4.5)

which come from gluing the edges of the root face of maps with root face degree
2
.

The generating functions of disk partition functions are usually defined formally as follows:

\displaystyle W_{\mathbf{q}}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\geqslant 0}W^{(l)}_{\mathbf{q}}\,z^{-(l+1)},

(4.6)

\displaystyle W_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\geqslant 0}W^{(l)}_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}\,z^{-(l+1)},

(4.7)

with the convention that
W^{(0)}_{\mathbf{q}}=W^{(0)}_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}=1
.
A striking property of Boltzmann maps is the universal form of the pointed disk function
W_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}
(see for instance Budd
[
23
, Proposition 2]
and Borot-Bouttier-Guitter
[
15
, Section 6.1]
for two different proofs). Indeed, there exist real numbers
c_{+}(\mathbf{q})>2
and
c_{-}(\mathbf{q})\in[-c_{+}(\mathbf{q}),c_{+}(\mathbf{q}))
such that
W_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}
has an analytic continuation on
\mathbb{C}\setminus[c_{-}(\mathbf{q}),c_{+}(\mathbf{q})]
and such that, on this domain, we have:

W_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left((z-c_{+}(\mathbf{q}))(z-c_{-}(\mathbf{q}))\right)}}.

(4.8)

In addition, if the maps are not bipartite (which is the case here), then
c_{-}(\mathbf{q})\neq-c_{+}(\mathbf{q})
. The unpointed disk function does not have a universal form, but it does have an analytic continuation on the same domain
\mathbb{C}\setminus[c_{-}(\mathbf{q}),c_{+}(\mathbf{q})]
than the pointed disk function (see for example
[
15
, Section 6.1]
and
[
10
, Proposition 4.3]
).

4.1.2
Critical and regular critical weight sequences

Weight sequences are classified according to their asymptotic properties.
The case where the number of vertices of a
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann map has infinite variance is of special interest, which leads to the following definition.

Definition 4.2
.

An admissible weight sequence
\mathbf{q}
is said to be

•

critical
if

\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}}\,|V(\mathfrak{m})|^{2}\,\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}=+\infty,

(4.9)

•

sub-critical
if

\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}}\,|V(\mathfrak{m})|^{2}\,\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}<+\infty.

(4.10)

Critical weight sequences are further classified according to the tail distribution of the degree of the root face of the associated
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann map. This classification can be found in
[
60
, Definition 1 and Proposition 3]
.

Definition 4.3
.

A critical weight sequence
\mathbf{q}
is said to be

•

regular
(or
generic
) critical if there exists
r\in(0,1)
such that, as
l\to\infty
, one has

\mathbb{P}^{\mathbf{q}}\left(|\partial\mathbf{M}^{\mathbf{q}}|=l\right)=\frac{w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M}_{l})}{w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M})}=\frac{q_{l}\cdot W_{\mathbf{q}}^{l}}{w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M})}=\mathcal{O}\left(r^{l}\right);

(4.11)

•

non-regular
(or
non-generic
) critical if it is not regular critical.

An important feature of regular critical Boltzmann random maps is the universal behavior for the tail distribution of their number of vertices. We refer the interested reader to
[
10
]
for further details and also to
[
23
, Lemma 2]
for a related result.

Proposition 4.4

(Proposition 5.1 of
[
10
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{q}
be a regular critical weight sequence. Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\mathbb{P}^{\mathbf{q}}\left(|V(\mathbf{M}^{\mathbf{q}})|\geqslant n\right)\underset{n\to\infty}{\sim}c\,n^{-3/2}.

(4.12)

4.1.3
Bouttier-Di Francesco-Guitter functions

An essential tool to study Boltzmann planar maps is the Bouttier-Di Francesco-Guitter bijection
[
20
]
. Our work relies heavily on one of its consequences established in slight variations by Miermont
[
60
]
, Budd
[
23
]
or Bernardi, Curien and Miermont
[
10
]
.
For a weight sequence
\mathbf{q}
, they give a criterion for
\mathbf{q}
to be admissible, and if applicable, they give an expression for the partition function of
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann maps.

More precisely, let
f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}}
and
f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}}
be the two bivariate functions defined by:

\displaystyle f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\geqslant 0}z_{1}^{k}z_{2}^{k^{\prime}}\binom{2k+k^{\prime}+1}{k+1,k,k^{\prime}}q_{2+2k+k^{\prime}},

(4.13)

\displaystyle f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\geqslant 0}z_{1}^{k}z_{2}^{k^{\prime}}\binom{2k+k^{\prime}}{k,k,k^{\prime}}q_{1+2k+k^{\prime}}.

(4.14)

It is proved in these three articles that the weight sequence
\mathbf{q}
is admissible if and only if there is a unique solution
(z^{+}(\mathbf{q}),z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}))\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
to the system

\begin{cases}f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}}(z^{+}(\mathbf{q}),z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}))=1-\frac{1}{z^{+}(\mathbf{q})},\\
f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}}(z^{+}(\mathbf{q}),z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}))=z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}),\\
(\partial_{z_{2}}+\sqrt{z_{1}}\partial_{z_{1}})f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}}(z^{+}(\mathbf{q}),z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}))\leqslant 1.\end{cases}

(4.15)

In addition, if
\mathbf{q}
is admissible, the solution of the system has the following combinatorial interpretation

\begin{cases}z^{+}(\mathbf{q})&=1+\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{+}}\,\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}=1+w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M}^{+}),\\
z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q})&=\sqrt{\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{0}}\,\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}}=\sqrt{w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M}^{0})},\end{cases}

(4.16)

where
\mathcal{M}^{+}
(respectively
\mathcal{M}^{0}
) denotes the subset of
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
made of maps whose root edge points to a vertex that is closer (respectively at equal distance) to the marked vertex than is the root vertex. See for example
[
10
, Lemma 4.4]
. As a consequence, we have

w_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=2z^{+}(\mathbf{q})+(z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}))^{2}-1,

(4.17)

or, equivalently,

c_{+}(\mathbf{q})=z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q})+2\sqrt{z^{+}}(\mathbf{q})\quad\text{and}\quad\quad c_{-}(\mathbf{q})=z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q})-2\sqrt{z^{+}}(\mathbf{q}).

(4.18)

Based on these results, the following characterization of criticality is given in

[
10
]
:

Proposition 4.5

(Proposition 4.3 of
[
10
]
)
.

For
\mathbf{q}
an admissible weight sequence, the three following statements are equivalent:

•

\mathbf{q}
is sub-critical (in the sense given in Definition
4.2
),

•

the inequality in the third equation of (
4.15
) is strict,

•

its disk partition functions have the following asymptotic behavior: there exist constants
c,R>0
such that:

W_{\mathbf{q}}^{(l)}\underset{l\to\infty}{\sim}c\,R^{l}\,l^{-3/2}.

(4.19)

Finally, the functions
f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}}
and
f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}}
also give indirect access to the
volume generating functions
of
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann planar maps. Indeed, for
0<g\leqslant 1
consider the weight sequence
\mathbf{q}_{g}
defined by
\mathbf{q}_{g}(k)=g^{(k-2)/2}q_{k}
for
k\geqslant 1
. Euler’s formula ensures that

g\,w_{\mathbf{q}_{g}}(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}}\,|V(\mathfrak{m})|\,g^{|V(\mathfrak{m})|-1}\,\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}.

Following
[
10
]
, and by analogy with (
4.16
), we define

\begin{cases}z^{+}(\mathbf{q},g)&=g\left(1+w_{\mathbf{q}_{g}}(\mathcal{M}^{+})\right),\\
z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},g)&=\sqrt{g\,w_{\mathbf{q}_{g}}(\mathcal{M}^{0})},\end{cases}

(4.20)

so that we have:

\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}}\,|V(\mathfrak{m})|\,g^{|V(\mathfrak{m})|-1}\,\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}=2(z^{+}(\mathbf{q},g)-g)+(z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},g))^{2},

(4.21)

and

c_{\pm}(\mathbf{q}_{g})=\frac{z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},g)\pm 2\sqrt{z^{+}(\mathbf{q},g)}}{\sqrt{g}}.

(4.22)

From their definition,
z^{+}(\mathbf{q},g)
and
z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},g)
are easily seen to be increasing in
g
. Besides, by monotone convergence their limit as
g\to 1^{-}
are given by
z^{+}(\mathbf{q},1)=z^{+}(\mathbf{q})
and
z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},1)=z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q})
.
On the other hand, we can easily check that for
g\in(0,1]
and
x,y>0
we have

f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}_{g}}(x,y)=f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}}(g\,x,\sqrt{g}\,y)\quad\text{and}\quad f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}_{g}}(x,y)=f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}}(g\,x,\sqrt{g}\,y)/\sqrt{g}.

(4.23)

Therefore, by first applying (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) to
f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}_{g}}
and
f^{\circ}_{\mathbf{q}_{g}}
, then by replacing
w_{\mathbf{q}_{g}}(\mathcal{M}^{+})
and
w_{\mathbf{q}_{g}}(\mathcal{M}^{0})
by their expression in terms of
z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},g)
and of
z^{+}(\mathbf{q},g)
, and by using (
4.23
), we obtain that for
0<g\leqslant 1
the quantities
0<z^{+}(\mathbf{q},g)\leqslant z^{+}(\mathbf{q})
and
0<z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},g)\leqslant z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q})
are the unique solutions of:

\begin{cases}f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}}(z^{+}(\mathbf{q},g),z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},g))&=1-\frac{g}{z^{+}(\mathbf{q},g))},\\
f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}}(z^{+}(\mathbf{q},g),z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},g))&=z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},g).\end{cases}

(4.24)

4.1.4
Maps of the cylinder

We now turn our attention to the generating function of Boltzmann maps of the cylinder, that is maps with two marked faces. More precisely, for
l_{1},l_{2}>0
, let
\mathcal{M}_{(l_{1},l_{2})}
be the set of all finite maps with two boundary faces
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
of respective perimeter
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
, and rooted at each boundary in such a way that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
lie on the right side of the corresponding root. The partition function of
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann maps in
\mathcal{M}_{(l_{1},l_{2})}
is defined by:

W_{\mathbf{q},2}^{(l_{1},l_{2})}=\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{(l_{1},l_{2})}}\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})\setminus\{f_{1},f_{2}\}}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}

(4.25)

and their generating series by

W_{\mathbf{q},2}(z_{1},z_{2})=\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}\geqslant 1}\frac{W_{\mathbf{q},2}^{(l_{1},l_{2})}}{z_{1}^{l_{1}+1}z_{2}^{l_{2}+1}}.

The expression of
W_{\mathbf{q},2}
is well known and is given by:

Proposition 4.6
.

The generating series
W_{\mathbf{q},2}(z_{1},z_{2})
of
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann planar maps of the cylinder admits the following expression:

W_{\mathbf{q},2}(z_{1},z_{2})\\
=\frac{1}{2(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}\left(W_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}(z_{1})W_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}(z_{2})\left(z_{1}z_{2}-\frac{c_{+}(\mathbf{q})+c_{-}(\mathbf{q})}{2}(z_{1}+z_{2})+c_{+}(\mathbf{q})c_{-}(\mathbf{q})\right)-1\right).

(4.26)

This formula was originally obtained in the physics literature via some connections with matrix integrals, see
[
3
]
. It also appears in
[
16
, Theorem 3.3]
and in the recent book by Eynard
[
34
, Theorem 3.2.1]
, but is given via a rational parametrization. We give in Appendix
A
, the connection between the expression given in (
4.26
) and Eynard’s formula. For the sake of completeness, we also give a fully combinatorial derivation of this proposition based on the theory of slice decomposition introduced by Bouttier and Guitter
[
22
,
21
]
.

4.2
The root spin cluster is a
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)
-Boltzmann map

The main point of this section is to prove that the root spin cluster of an Ising-weighted triangulation is a Boltzmann map, whose weight sequence is the sequence
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)
defined in the introduction. As a byproduct, we will see that this weight sequence is always admissible when
\nu>0
and
0<t\leqslant t_{\nu}
. We will then study the singular behavior in
t
and
y
of these weights.

4.2.1
Decomposition of finite triangulations into the root spin cluster and islands

Recall that
\mathcal{T}^{\oplus}
is the set of Ising-weighted triangulations with a positive monochromatic root edge. For
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{\oplus}
, we also recall that its root spin cluster – denoted by
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
– is the submap of
\mathfrak{t}
spanned by all the vertices with spin
\oplus
connected to the root by a sequence of monochromatic edges, with the same root edge as
\mathfrak{t}
. The root spin cluster of a triangulation of
\mathcal{T}^{\oplus}
can thus be seen as a finite rooted non atomic map without spins.

To characterize the probability distribution of the root spin cluster of a triangulation with spins
\mathbf{T}^{\nu}
sampled from
\mathbb{P}^{\nu}
, we decompose
\mathbf{T}^{\nu}
into its root spin cluster and some submaps filling its faces. As mentioned in the introduction, such decompositions are now classical and appear in the literature under several aliases: gasket decomposition in
[
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
or reef-island decomposition in
[
10
]
. We start by introducing the weight sequence:

Definition 4.7
.

For every
\nu>0
and every positive integer
k
, we define for
|t|\leqslant t_{\nu}

q_{k}(\nu,t)=\left(\nu\,t\right)^{k/2}\mathbf{1}_{\{k=3\}}+\left(\nu\,t^{3}\right)^{k/2}\cdot\sum_{l\geqslant 0}\binom{k+l-1}{k-1}\,t^{l}\,Q_{l}^{+}(\nu,t),

(4.27)

and set
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)=\left(q_{k}(\nu,t)\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
.

Notice that the generating series of this weight sequence is the series
F(\nu,t,z)
introduced in (
1.6
) in the introduction. Indeed, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geqslant 1}q_{k}(\nu,t)z^{k}

\displaystyle=(\nu t)^{3/2}z^{3}+\sum_{l\geqslant 0}t^{l}\,Q_{l}^{+}(\nu,t)\,\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\binom{k+l-1}{k-1}\left(\nu\,t^{3}\right)^{k/2}z^{k},

\displaystyle=(\nu t)^{3/2}z^{3}+\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z\,\sum_{l\geqslant 0}t^{l}\,Q_{l}^{+}(\nu,t)\cdot(1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z)^{-(l+1)},

\displaystyle=(\nu t)^{3/2}z^{3}+\frac{\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z}{1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z}\,Q^{+}\left(\nu,t,\frac{t}{1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z}\right),

\displaystyle=F(\nu,t,z).

The next statement relates the weight sequence
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)
with the partition function of the root spin cluster:

Proposition 4.8
.

Fix
\nu>0
and
t\in(0,t_{\nu}]
.
For any
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}
, we have:

\sum_{(\mathfrak{t},\sigma)\in\mathcal{T}^{\oplus}\,:\,\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})=\mathfrak{m}}t^{|\mathfrak{t}|}\nu^{m(\mathfrak{t},\sigma)}=\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}(\nu,t).

As a consequence, we have the following identity:

\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}(\nu,t)=\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}}\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}(\nu,t)<\infty,

where
\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}
is defined in (
2.13
). In particular, the weight sequence
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)
is admissible.

Before giving the proof of this proposition, we state the following characterization of the root spin cluster as a
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)
-Boltzmann map, which is at the heart of our analysis and is one of its direct consequences:

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}
and fix
\nu>0
.

•

Unconditioned volume case: The law of the root spin cluster
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbf{T}^{\nu})
is given by

\mathbb{P}^{\nu}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathbf{T}^{\nu})=\mathfrak{m}\right)=\frac{\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}(\nu,t_{\nu})}{\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}(\nu,t_{\nu})}.

(4.28)

In other words,
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbf{T}^{\nu})
is distributed as a
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu})
-Boltzmann map
.

•

Conditioned volume case: The law of the root spin cluster
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbf{T}^{\nu}_{n})
is given by

\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\nu}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathbf{T}_{n}^{\nu})=\mathfrak{m}\right)=\frac{[t^{3n}]\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}(\nu,t)}{[t^{3n}]\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}(\nu,t)}.

(4.29)

(a)
An element of
\mathcal{T}^{\oplus},

(b)
and its root spin cluster. Letters
A
and
B
refer to Figures
7c
and
7d
.

(c)
Island (left) and necklace (right) corresponding to the face
A
in
7b
.

(d)
Island (left) and necklace (right) corresponding to the face
B
in
7b
.

Figure 7:
Example of the root spin cluster of an Ising configuration, see
7b
, and of the necklace/island decomposition, see
7c
and
7d

Proof of Proposition
4.8
.

Following
[
13
]
and
[
10
]
, for any
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{\oplus}
, we consider the following decomposition of
\mathfrak{t}
. Let
C
be the cut-set of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
, that is the set of edges of
\mathfrak{t}
that have one endpoint in
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
and the other endpoint not in
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
. It follows directly from the definition of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
, that every edge in
C
is frustrated (
i.e.
not monochromatic). Next, the connected components of
\mathfrak{t}\backslash C
are
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
and a collection of maps (eventually atomic) – called
islands
– with non simple boundaries, and such that their boundary vertices have all spin
\ominus
, see Figure
7
. Furthermore, each face of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
that is not of degree
3
contains exactly one of these islands. Faces of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
with degree
3
can contain either an island or nothing if it is also a face of the original triangulation
\mathfrak{t}
.

Reciprocally, suppose that we are given a cluster
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
and a collection of maps with spins, indexed by the faces of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
(except possibly for some faces of degree 3). Assume that these maps have non simple boundaries (eventually atomic) with monochromatic
\ominus
boundary condition.

The only missing information to recover the full triangulation is the edges between vertices of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
and the maps inside each of its faces. These parts are called reefs in
[
10
]
or necklaces in
[
13
]
. The part between the boundary of a face of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})
of degree
k
and the corresponding island with boundary length
l
is therefore a map with a root face of degree
k
, a marked inner face of degree
l
such that these two faces do not share vertices. Moreover, vertices in this map are only incident either to the root face or to the marked inner face.
In addition, every other face of this map is a triangle. Thus, there are
k+l
such triangles, among them
k
share an edge with the root face and their third vertex with the marked face (if it is non empty), and
l
share a edge with the marked face and their third vertex with the root face. There are
\binom{k+l-1}{k-1}
such necklaces (this is the number of different orderings of the two types of triangles). See Figures
7
and
7
for an illustration.

Summing over every possible island and reef, this decomposition yields the following identity for any fixed non atomic map
\mathfrak{m}
:

\sum_{(\mathfrak{t},\sigma)\in\mathcal{T}^{\oplus}\,:\,\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})=\mathfrak{m}}t^{|\mathfrak{t}|}\nu^{m(\mathfrak{t},\sigma)}=t^{|\mathfrak{m}|}\nu^{|\mathfrak{m}|}\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{deg}(f)=3}+\sum_{l\geqslant 0}\binom{\mathrm{deg}(f)+l-1}{\mathrm{deg}(f)-1}\,t^{\mathrm{deg}(f)+l}\,Q_{l}^{+}(\nu,t)\right)

where the term
\mathbf{1}_{k=3}
takes into account the fact that a face of degree
3
in
\mathfrak{m}
could be a face of the original triangulation
\mathfrak{t}
. In view of the definition of
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)
given in Definition
4.7
, this identity can immediately be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathfrak{t},\sigma)\in\mathcal{T}^{\oplus}\,:\,\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{t})=\mathfrak{m}}t^{|\mathfrak{t}|}\nu^{m(\mathfrak{t},\sigma)}=\prod_{f\in F(\mathfrak{m})}q_{\mathrm{deg}(f)}(\nu,t)

(4.30)

The last statement of the proposition follows from the definition of
\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}(\nu,t)
and the fact that
\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}(\nu,t_{\nu})<\infty
as stated in Proposition
2.5
.
∎

4.2.2
Disk generating functions of
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)
-Boltzmann maps

As mentioned in Section
4.1.1
, the unpointed disk function defined in (
4.6
) does not have a universal form. Fortunately, for
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)
-Boltzmann maps it can be computed explicitly:

Proposition 4.10
.

Fix
\nu>0
and
t\in(0,t_{\nu}]
, the disk generating function associated to
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)
is given by

W_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}(z)=\frac{1}{z}Q^{+}\left(\nu,t,\frac{t}{z\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}}\right).

As a consequence, for every
\nu>0
, when
t=t_{\nu}
, the weight sequence
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu})
is critical in the sense given in (
4.9
).

Proof.

Consider a triangulation
\mathfrak{t}
endowed with an Ising configuration and with a
monochromatic
boundary of length
l
. When we apply the gasket decomposition presented in Section
4.2.1
to
\mathfrak{t}
, we obtain a map of
\mathcal{M}_{l}
.
By following the same line of arguments as in Proposition
4.8
, we see that:

W^{(l)}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}=Q_{l}^{+}(\nu,t)\cdot(\nu t)^{-l/2},

(4.31)

where the corrective term
(\nu t)^{-l/2}
comes from the fact that the root face does not contribute to
W^{(l)}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}
. Therefore each edge incident to the root face contributes only a weight
\sqrt{\nu t}
to
W^{(l)}
, whereas it contributes a weight
\nu t
to
Q_{l}^{+}(\nu,t)
.

The generating series is then easy to compute:

\displaystyle W_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\geqslant 0}Q_{l}^{+}(\nu,t)\cdot(\nu t)^{-l/2}z^{-(l+1)},

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\geqslant 0}t^{l}Q_{l}^{+}(\nu,t)\cdot(\nu t^{3})^{-l/2}z^{-(l+1)},

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{z}Q^{+}\left(\nu,t,\frac{t}{z\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}}\right).

To see that the weight sequence
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu})
is critical, we apply the criticality criterion given in Proposition
4.5
, where we rely on the asymptotic behavior of
Q^{+}_{l}(\nu,t_{\nu})
, as
l
grows to infinity, given in Corollary
3.11
.
∎

Since the function
W_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}
always has an analytic continuation on the same domain as the pointed function
W_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t),\bullet}
defined in (
4.7
), this expression allows us to compute
c_{+}(\nu,t):=c_{+}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t))
and
c_{-}(\nu,t):=c_{-}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t))
in terms of the singularities in y of
Q^{+}(t,ty)
appearing in Proposition
3.15
. We obtain:

Corollary 4.11
.

The pointed disk function
W_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t),\bullet}
defined in (
4.7
) admits the following expression:

W_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t),\bullet}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Big(z-c_{+}(\nu,t)\Big)\Big(z-c_{-}(\nu,t)\Big)}},

(4.32)

where

c_{+}(\nu,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}\,y_{+}(\nu,t)}\quad\text{ and }\quad c_{-}(\nu,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}\,y_{-}(\nu,t)}.

(4.33)

Remark 4.12
.

Since the maps we consider are not bipartite, we have
c_{+}(\nu,t)>-c_{-}(\nu,t)
(this is a general property of non-bipartite Boltzmann maps recalled in Section
4.1.1
and which is a direct consequence of (
4.18
)). The expression of
c_{+}(\nu,t)
and
c_{-}(\nu,t)
given above then imply that

y_{-}(\nu,t)<-y_{+}(\nu,t),

(4.34)

as was announced in Proposition
3.7
.

The expressions for
c_{+}(\nu,t)
and
c_{-}(\nu,t)
in terms of
y_{+}(\nu,t)
and
y_{-}(\nu,t)
also give expressions for the solution
\left(z^{+}(\nu,t),z^{\diamond}(\nu,t)\right):=\left(z^{+}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)),z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t))\right)
of the system of equations (
4.15
):

\displaystyle z^{+}(\nu,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{+}(\nu,t)}+\frac{1}{y_{-}(\nu,t)}\right),

(4.35)

\displaystyle z^{\diamond}(\nu,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\,\nu t^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{+}(\nu,t)}-\frac{1}{y_{-}(\nu,t)}\right)^{2}.

(4.36)

4.2.3
Asymptotic properties of the weights
q_{k}(\nu,t)

We gather in this section some asymptotic properties of the weight sequence
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)
. We start with the special case where
t=t_{\nu}
:

Proposition 4.13
.

The weight sequence
(q_{k}(\nu,t_{\nu}))_{k\geqslant 1}
has the following asymptotic behavior:

q_{k}(\nu,t_{\nu})\underset{k\to\infty}{\sim}\frac{(y_{\nu}-1)^{a(\nu)}\,\aleph^{Q^{+}}(\nu)}{\Gamma(1-a(\nu))}\,\left(\frac{y_{\nu}}{y_{\nu}-1}\,\sqrt{\nu t_{\nu}^{3}}\right)^{k}k^{-a(\nu)},

where we recall that
y_{\nu}
and
a(\nu)
were first defined in Proposition
3.10
and satisfy:

\begin{cases}y_{\nu}=2\text{ and }a(\nu)=5/3&\text{for }\nu<\nu_{c}\\
y_{\nu_{c}}=2\text{ and }a(\nu_{c})=7/3&\text{for }\nu=\nu_{c}\\
y_{\nu}>2\text{ and }a(\nu)=5/2&\text{for }\nu>\nu_{c}\end{cases}

Proof.

From the expression of the generating series
F(\nu,t_{\nu},z)
of the weights
q_{k}(\nu,t_{\nu})
, we can see that the asymptotic behavior of
q_{k}(\nu,t_{\nu})
as
k
grows to infinity is driven (up to a change of variable) by the singular behavior (in y) of
Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},y)
.

We established in Proposition
3.6
that,
y\mapsto Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},ty)
has two singularities, namely
y_{\nu}=y_{+}(\nu,t_{\nu})
and
y_{-}(\nu,t_{\nu})
. The possible singularities
z_{\nu}
and
z_{-}(\nu)
of
z\mapsto F(\nu,t_{\nu},z)
are then given by:

\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{1-\sqrt{\nu t_{\nu}^{3}}z_{\nu}}=y_{\nu}\,&\Leftrightarrow\,z_{\nu}=\dfrac{y_{\nu}-1}{\sqrt{\nu t_{\nu}^{3}}\,y_{\nu}}\\
\dfrac{1}{1-\sqrt{\nu t_{\nu}^{3}}z_{-}(\nu)}=y_{-}(\nu,t_{\nu})\,&\Leftrightarrow\,z_{-}(\nu)=\dfrac{y_{-}(\nu,t_{\nu})-1}{\sqrt{\nu t_{\nu}^{3}}\,y_{-}(\nu,t_{\nu})}.\end{cases}

(4.37)

Since for any
\nu>0
,
y_{\nu}>0
and
y_{-}(\nu,t_{\nu})<0
, we have
0<z_{\nu}<z_{-}(\nu)
, so that
z_{\nu}
is the unique dominant singularity of
F(\nu,t_{\nu},z)
.
In addition, from the singular expansion of
y\mapsto Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}\,y)
at
y_{\nu}
, we see that the dominant singular term in the expansion of
z\mapsto F(\nu,t_{\nu},z)
at
z_{\nu}
is of the form

\displaystyle(y_{\nu}-1)^{a(\nu)}\,\aleph^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\,\left(1-\frac{z}{z_{\nu}}\right)^{a(\nu)-1},

and the classical transfer theorem of Appendix
B.1
gives the statement.
∎

Next we establish the asymptotic behavior of the coefficients in
t
of the weight
q_{k}(\nu,t)
for fixed
k
:

Proposition 4.14
.

For every
k\geqslant 1
, we have:

\frac{[t^{3n}]\left((\nu t^{3})^{-k/2}q_{k}(\nu,t)\right)}{[t^{3n}]\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}(\nu,t)}\underset{n\to\infty}{\rightarrow}\delta_{k}(\nu),

(4.38)

where the generating series of the numbers
\delta_{k}(\nu)
is given by

\Delta(\nu,z)=\sum_{k}\delta_{k}(\nu)\,z^{k}=\frac{1}{\beth^{\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}}(\nu)}\,\frac{z}{1-z}\beth^{Q^{+}}\left(\nu,\frac{1}{1-z}\right).

Moreover, we recall that
\beth^{Q^{+}}(\nu,y)
, defined in Proposition
3.12
, seen as a series in
y
, has an explicit rational parametrization in terms of
V(\nu,U_{\nu},y)
.

Proof.

For
k\geqslant 1
, we set:

\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)=(\nu t^{3})^{-k/2}\left(q_{k}(\nu,t)-(\nu t)^{3/2}\mathbf{1}_{k=3}\right)

It follows directly from the definition of the weights
q_{k}(\nu,t)
that, for every
k
, the modified weight
\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)
is a series in
t^{3}
with nonnegative coefficients and radius of convergence
t_{\nu}^{3}
. By Pringsheim’s theorem,
\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)
is hence singular at
t_{\nu}^{3}
and we will prove that this is its unique dominant singularity.

From Proposition
4.8
and the expression of the generating series
F(\nu,t,z)
given in (
1.6
), we can write:

\tilde{F}(\nu,t,z)=\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)z^{k-1}=\frac{1}{1-z}\,Q^{+}\left(\nu,t,\frac{t}{1-z}\right).

(4.39)

From this expression and the algebraic equation (
3.1
) satisfied by
Q^{+}
,
\tilde{F}
can be expressed as the solution of an algebraic equation involving
\nu
,
t^{3}
and
tZ_{1}^{+}(\nu,t)
, see the Maple file
[
1
]
for details. Note that, by definition, the constant coefficient of
\tilde{F}
(as a formal power series in
z
) is equal to
Q^{+}(\nu,t,t)
. The algebraic equation satisfied by
\tilde{F}
takes the following form: There exists two explicit polynomials
\mathrm{Pol}_{1}
and
\mathrm{Pol}_{2}
such that:

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{F}(\nu,t,z)-Q^{+}(\nu,t,t)\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathrm{Pol}_{1}\left(\tilde{F}(\nu,t,z),Q^{+}(\nu,t,t),\nu,t^{3},tZ_{1}^{+}(\nu,t)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=z\cdot\mathrm{Pol}_{2}\left(z,\tilde{F}(\nu,t,z),\nu,t^{3},tZ_{1}^{+}(\nu,t))\right).

(4.40)

Furthermore,
\mathrm{Pol}_{1}\left(Q^{+}(\nu,t,t),Q^{+}(\nu,t,t),\nu,t^{3},tZ_{1}^{+}(\nu,t)\right)
is the derivative with respect to
Q^{+}(\nu,t,t)
of the algebraic equation satisfied by
Q^{+}(\nu,t,t)
and can be
0
only at the singularities (in
t^{3}
) of
Q^{+}(\nu,t,t)
, namely only for
t^{3}=t_{\nu}^{3}
. This implies that the coefficients
\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)
can be iteratively calculated from (
4.40
). In addition, each
\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)
has the following expression:

\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)=\frac{\mathrm{P}_{k}\left(Q^{+}(\nu,t,t),\nu,t^{3},tZ_{1}^{+}(\nu,t),(\tilde{q}_{l}(\nu,t))_{l<k}\right)}{\left(\mathrm{Pol}_{1}\left(Q^{+}(\nu,t,t),Q^{+}(\nu,t,t),\nu,t^{3},tZ_{1}^{+}(\nu,t)\right)\right)^{k}}

where
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
is a sequence of polynomials, which can be computed by induction. Since the denominator of this expression cannot be
0
for
|t^{3}|\leqslant t_{\nu}^{3}
and
t^{3}\neq t_{\nu}^{3}
, this implies that for every
k
,
\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)
seen as a series in
t^{3}
is algebraic and has a unique dominant singularity at
t^{3}_{\nu}
(recall that we already established that
t^{3}_{\nu}
is a singularity).

By the standard singular behavior of algebraic functions near their singularities (see for instance
[
36
, Theorem VII.7 page 198]
), we know that
[t^{3n}]\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)
has an asymptotic behavior of the form
\mathrm{Cst}(k)\,t_{\nu}^{-3n}\,n^{r_{k}(\nu)}
, where
r_{k}(\nu)\in\mathbb{Q}
for any
\nu
. We can easily squeeze
[t^{3n}]\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)
between two bounds of the same order to identify
r_{k}
. Indeed from the definition of the weights
q_{k}(\nu,t)
:

[t^{3n}]tZ_{1}^{+}(\nu,t)\leqslant[t^{3n}]\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)\leqslant[t^{3(n-k)}]\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}(\nu,t).

This implies that for every
k
we have
r_{k}(\nu)=-5/2
for any
\nu\neq\nu_{c}
and
r_{k}(\nu_{c})=-7/3
, i.e. for any
k
,
r_{k}
is equal to the function
\gamma
defined in (
2.12
) of Proposition
2.4
.
This finishes to establish the asymptotic behavior of
[t^{3n}]\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)
given in (
4.38
). It remains to identify the generating series of the numbers
\delta_{k}(\nu)
.

For every
k
, the asymptotic expansion of
\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)
is of the form:

\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t)=\tilde{q}_{k}(\nu,t_{\nu})+\beth^{\tilde{q}_{k}}_{1}(\nu)\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)+\beth^{\tilde{q}_{k}}(\nu)\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}+o\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}.

(4.41)

Recall from Proposition
2.5
that we also have

\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}(\nu,t)=\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}(\nu,t_{\nu})+\beth^{\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}}_{1}(\nu)\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)+\beth^{\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}}(\nu)\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}+o\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}.

Therefore, for every
k\geqslant 1
:

\delta_{k}(\nu)=\frac{\beth^{\tilde{q}_{k}}(\nu)}{\beth^{\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}}(\nu)}.

We define the formal power series in
z
:

\beth^{\tilde{q}}_{1}(\nu,z)=\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\beth^{\tilde{q}_{k}}_{1}(\nu)\,z^{k}\quad\text{and}\quad\beth^{\tilde{q}}(\nu,z)=\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\beth^{\tilde{q}_{k}}(\nu)\,z^{k}.

By definition, the generating series
\Delta(\nu,z)
of the lemma is the formal power series
\beth^{\tilde{q}}(\nu,z)/\beth^{\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}}(\nu)
. Therefore, we want to show that, as a generating series in z, we have

{\beth^{\tilde{q}}(\nu,z)}=\frac{z}{1-z}\,\beth^{Q^{+}}\left(\nu,\frac{1}{1-z}\right).

Informally, it boils down to proving that the development of
Q^{+}
coincides with the sum of the developments of the
\tilde{q}_{k}
. We cannot sum them directly since the error term in (
4.41
) is
a priori
not uniform in
k
. However, by an approach similar to the proof of
[
2
, Lemma 5.1]
, we can establish this result.

Indeed, for every
t
such that
|t|\leqslant|t_{\nu}|
, the radius of convergence in
y
of
Q^{+}(\nu,t,ty)
is at least
y_{\nu}\geqslant 2
. Fix
z
such that
|1/(1-z)|<y_{\nu}
, or equivalently
|z|<1-1/y_{\nu}
. From Proposition
3.12
, the series
\tilde{F}(\nu,t,z)
seen as a series in
t^{3}
is analytic, has radius of convergence
t_{\nu}^{3}
, and has an asymptotic expansion at
t_{\nu}^{3}
given by:

\displaystyle\tilde{F}(\nu,t,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-z}Q^{+}\left(t_{\nu},\frac{t_{\nu}}{1-z}\right)+\frac{1}{1-z}\,\beth^{Q^{+}}_{1}\left(\nu,\frac{1}{1-z}\right)\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{1-z}\,\beth^{Q^{+}}\left(\nu,\frac{1}{1-z}\right)\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1}+o\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)^{\gamma(\nu)-1},

where
\beth^{Q^{+}}_{1}(\nu,y)
and
\beth^{Q^{+}}(\nu,y)
are explicit rational functions of
\nu
and
V(\nu,U(t^{3}_{\nu}),y)
. Since
V(\nu,U(t^{3}_{\nu},1/(1-z))
is analytic for
|z|<1-1/y_{\nu}
, so are
\beth^{Q^{+}}_{1}(\nu,1/(1-z))
and
\beth^{Q^{+}}(\nu,1/(1-z))
. In particular for
|z|<1-1/y_{\nu}
, the applications
z\mapsto\beth^{Q^{+}}_{1}(\nu,1/(1-z))
and
z\mapsto\beth^{Q^{+}}(\nu,1/(1-z))
are equal to their respective developments in power series.
Even if the error term in the expansion of
\tilde{F}(\nu,t,z))
is not uniform in
z
, the power functions
\beth^{Q^{+}}_{1}
and
\beth^{Q^{+}}
can be seen as the following limits as formal power series:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-z}\,\beth^{Q^{+}}_{1}\left(\nu,\frac{1}{1-z}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to t_{\nu}^{-}}\left(\tilde{F}(\nu,t,z)-\frac{1}{1-z}Q^{+}\left(t_{\nu},\frac{t_{\nu}}{1-z}\right)\right)\cdot\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-z}\,\beth^{Q^{+}}\left(\nu,\frac{1}{1-z}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to t_{\nu}^{-}}\left(\tilde{F}(\nu,t,z)-\frac{1}{1-z}Q^{+}\left(t_{\nu},\frac{t_{\nu}}{1-z}\right)-\beth^{Q^{+}}_{1}\left(\nu,\frac{1}{1-z}\right)\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(1-(t/t_{\nu})^{3}\right)^{1-\gamma(\nu)}.

As a consequence,

\beth^{\tilde{q}}_{1}(\nu,z)=\frac{z}{1-z}\,\beth^{Q^{+}}_{1}\left(\nu,\frac{1}{1-z}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\beth^{\tilde{q}}(\nu,z)=\frac{z}{1-z}\,\beth^{Q^{+}}\left(\nu,\frac{1}{1-z}\right)

in their respective domains of analycity, which includes the open disk
|z|<1-1/y_{\nu}
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

We get as an immediate corollary the following asymptotic behavior of the numbers
\delta_{k}(\nu)
in the regime
\nu\leqslant\nu_{c}
.

Corollary 4.15
.

For
\nu\leqslant\nu_{c}
one has

\delta_{k}(\nu)\underset{k\to\infty}{\sim}\frac{1}{\beth^{\mathcal{Z}^{\oplus}}(\nu)}\frac{\aleph_{4/3}^{\beth^{Q^{+}}}(\nu)}{2^{4/3}\,\Gamma(4/3)}\,2^{k}k^{1/3}.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the expression of
\Delta
in terms of
\beth^{Q^{+}}
, of Proposition
3.14
giving the singular behavior of
\beth^{Q^{+}}
and of the transfer theorem of Appendix
B.1
.
∎

4.3
Computations around the BDG functions
f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}
and
f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}

The purpose of this section is to establish some preliminary and technical results that will be useful to compute the volume generating function of the root spin cluster of random Ising-weighted triangulations.

The main tool at our disposal to compute this generating function is its expression in terms of
z^{+}(\mathbf{q},g)
and
z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q},g)
given in (
4.21
). In order to be able to use this expression, we need to calculate the development of
z^{+}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu}),g)
and
z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu}),g)
around
z^{+}_{\nu}
and
z^{\diamond}_{\nu}
respectively (or, in other words, when
g
goes to 1).

We will proceed in three steps. In a first section, we obtain an explicit integral formula for
f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}
and
f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}
. Thanks to this expression, we can compute the singular development of these functions around
(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})
. Finally, we use this development together with the fact that
z^{+}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu}),g)
and
z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu}),g)
are solutions of a system of equations involving
f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}
and
f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}
, to get the desired expansions.

4.3.1
Integral expressions for
f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}
and
f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}

The functions
f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}}
and
f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}}
defined in (
4.13
) and (
4.14
) of Section
4.1.3
are usually hard to compute for non bipartite maps. However, in our setting, we are able to obtain an integral expression involving
Q^{+}
for
f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}
and
f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}
:

Proposition 4.16
.

Fix
\nu>0
and
t\in(0,t_{\nu}]
. For any
z_{1}\in[0,z^{+}(\nu,t)]
and
z_{2}\in[0,z^{\diamond}(\nu,t)]
we have

\displaystyle f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=(\nu t)^{3/2}\,2z_{2}+\frac{\nu t^{3}}{\pi}\int_{{1}/{a_{-}}}^{{1}/{a_{+}}}\,\frac{1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2}-\frac{1}{z}}{2\nu t^{3}z_{1}}\,\frac{Q^{+}(\nu,t,tz)}{\sqrt{\left(1-za_{+}\right)\left(za_{-}-1\right)}}\,\mathrm{d}z,

(4.42)

\displaystyle f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=(\nu t)^{3/2}\,(2z_{1}+z_{2}^{2})+\frac{\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}}{\pi}\int_{1/a_{-}}^{1/a_{+}}\,\frac{Q^{+}(\nu,t,tz)}{\sqrt{\left(1-za_{+}\right)\left(za_{-}-1\right)}}\,\mathrm{d}z,

(4.43)

where we set:

\displaystyle a_{+}:=1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}})\quad\text{and}\quad a_{-}:=1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}-2\sqrt{z_{1}}).

(4.44)

Proof.

First, plugging the value of the weights
q_{l}(\nu,t)
in the definition of the two functions gives the following identities:

\displaystyle f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=2(\nu t)^{3/2}z_{2}+\nu t^{3}\,\sum_{k,k^{\prime},l\geqslant 0}\binom{2k+k^{\prime}+l+1}{k+1,k,k^{\prime},l}\cdot t^{l}Q_{l}^{+}(\nu,t)\cdot(\nu t^{3}\,z_{1})^{k}(\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2})^{k^{\prime}},

(4.45)

\displaystyle f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=(\nu t)^{3/2}(2z_{1}+z_{2}^{2})+\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}\,\sum_{k,k^{\prime},l\geqslant 0}\binom{2k+k^{\prime}+l}{k,k,k^{\prime},l}\cdot t^{l}Q_{l}^{+}(\nu,t)\cdot(\nu t^{3}\,z_{1})^{k}(\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2})^{k^{\prime}}.

(4.46)

Fix
\nu>0
,
t\in(0,t_{\nu}]
, and then
z_{1}\in(0,z^{+}(\nu,t)]
and
z_{2}\in(0,z^{\diamond}(\nu,t)]
. The sums appearing in the previous displays can be expressed as Hadamard products, see Appendix
B.2
. Indeed, define the two formal power series
g^{\bullet}(z)
and
g^{\circ}(z)
as:

\displaystyle g^{\bullet}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime},l\geqslant 0}\binom{2k+k^{\prime}+l+1}{k+1,k,k^{\prime},l}(\nu t^{3}\,z_{1})^{k}(\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2})^{k^{\prime}}\cdot z^{l},

\displaystyle g^{\diamond}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime},l\geqslant 0}\binom{2k+k^{\prime}+l}{k,k,k^{\prime},l}(\nu t^{3}\,z_{1})^{k}(\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2})^{k^{\prime}}\cdot z^{l}.

Then

\displaystyle f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=2(\nu t)^{3/2}z_{2}+\nu t^{3}\,Q^{+}(\nu,t,tz)\odot g^{\bullet}(z)|_{z=1},

(4.47)

\displaystyle f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t)}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=(\nu t)^{3/2}(2z_{1}+z_{2}^{2})+\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}\,Q^{+}(\nu,t,tz)\odot g^{\diamond}(z)|_{z=1}.

(4.48)

Now let us compute the functions
g^{\bullet}
and
g^{\diamond}
.
We first rewrite:

g^{\bullet}(z)=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\geqslant 0}\binom{2k+k^{\prime}+1}{k+1,k,k^{\prime}}(\nu t^{3}\,z_{1})^{k}(\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2})^{k^{\prime}}\sum_{l\geqslant 0}\binom{2k+k^{\prime}+l+1}{l}z^{l}.

Then, if
|z|<1
, summing over
l
yields

g^{\bullet}(z)=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\geqslant 0}\binom{2k+k^{\prime}+1}{k+1,k,k^{\prime}}(\nu t^{3}\,z_{1})^{k}(\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2})^{k^{\prime}}\cdot(1-z)^{-(2k+k^{\prime}+2)}.

Similarly, if
|z|<1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2}
, which guarantees
|\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2}/(1-z)|<1
, summing over
k^{\prime}
yields

g^{\bullet}(z)=\sum_{k\geqslant 0}\binom{2k+1}{k+1}(\nu t^{3}\,z_{1})^{k}\left(1-z-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2}\right)^{-(2k+2)}.

Finally, for
|z|<1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}})
, which guarantees that
|\nu t^{3}\,z_{1}/(1-z-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2})^{2}|<1/4
, we get

\displaystyle g^{\bullet}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\nu t^{3}\,z_{1}}\left(\left(1-4\frac{\nu t^{3}\,z_{1}}{(1-z-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}\,z_{2})^{2}}\right)^{-1/2}-1\right),

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\nu t^{3}\,z_{1}}\left(\frac{1-z-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}z_{2}}{\sqrt{\left(1-z-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}})\right)\left(1-z-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}-2\sqrt{z_{1}})\right)}}-1\right).

A similar sequence of computations implies that for
|z|<1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}})
:

g^{\diamond}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left(1-z-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}})\right)\left(1-z-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}-2\sqrt{z_{1}})\right)}}.

Note that the region
|z|<1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}})
is not empty since
\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}})\leqslant\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}c_{+}(\nu,t)=\frac{1}{y_{+}(\nu,t)}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
.

The Hadamard product can be computed with an integral formula (see for instance
[
36
, Section VI.10.2]
or Appendix
B.2
). This yields:

Q^{+}(\nu,t,tz)\odot g^{\bullet}(z)|_{z=1}=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\gamma}Q^{+}(\nu,t,tw)\,g^{\bullet}\left(\frac{1}{w}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}w}{w},

and

Q^{+}(\nu,t,tz)\odot g^{\diamond}(z)|_{z=1}=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\gamma}Q^{+}(\nu,t,tw)\,g^{\diamond}\left(\frac{1}{w}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}w}{w},

where the integration contour
\gamma
in the
w
-plane should be chosen in such a way that both factors
Q^{+}(\nu,t,tw)
and
g^{\bullet}\left(\frac{1}{w}\right)
(or
g^{\diamond}\left(\frac{1}{w}\right)
) are analytic in an open neighborhood of
\gamma
.

Figure 8:
Integration contour for the Hadamard product.

By the above computations,
g^{\bullet}\left(\frac{1}{w}\right)
and
g^{\diamond}\left(\frac{1}{w}\right)
are analytic for

w\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Big[\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}-2\sqrt{z_{1}})},\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}})}\Big].

Furthermore, Proposition
3.6
ensures that
Q^{+}(\nu,t,tw)
is analytic for
w\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{(-\infty,y_{-}(\nu,t)]\cup[y_{+}(\nu,t),+\infty)\}
. For
z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}}<c_{+}(\nu,t)
, a suitable contour of integration then exists. Indeed, recalling that
y_{+}(\nu,t)\geqslant 2
, we have in this case

\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}})}<\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}c_{+}(\nu,t)}=\frac{y_{+}(\nu,t)}{y_{+}(\nu,t)-1}\leqslant 2,

and we also always have

\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}-2\sqrt{z_{1}})}>\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}c_{+}(\nu,t)}=\frac{y_{+}(\nu,t)}{y_{+}(\nu,t)+1}\geqslant\frac{2}{3}.

We can thus take
\gamma
inside the disk of convergence of
Q^{+}(\nu,t,tw)
and enclosing a cut
[1/(1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}-2\sqrt{z_{1}})),1/(1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}}))]
, see Figure
8
. Taking the limit when the curve
\gamma
approaches the cut then gives the expressions of the proposition for
z_{1}\in[0,z^{+}(\nu,t))
and
z_{2}\in[0,z^{\diamond}(\nu,t))
. The equality for
(z_{1},z_{2})=(z^{+}(\nu,t),z^{\diamond}(\nu,t))
follows by taking the limit.
∎

4.3.2
Singular developments of
f^{\bullet}_{\nu}
and
f^{\diamond}_{\nu}
around
(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})

Denote by
(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu}):=(z^{+}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu})),z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu})))
the solution of the system of equations (
4.15
). Recall from (
4.35
) and (
4.36
) that there exist explicit expressions for
z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu}
in terms of the singularities
y_{+}(\nu,t_{\nu})
and
y_{-}(\nu,t_{\nu})
of the function
y\mapsto Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}\,y)
, which are themselves explicit functions of
\nu
.

Using the expressions for
f^{\bullet}
and
f^{\diamond}
given in Proposition
4.16
and the singular behavior of
y\mapsto Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}\,y)
obtained in Proposition
3.10
, we can compute explicitly an asymptotic expansion of the BDG functions
f^{\bullet}
and
f^{\diamond}
at
(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})
:

Lemma 4.17
.

Fix
\nu\in(0,\nu_{c}]
and write
f^{\bullet}_{\nu}=f^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu})}
,
f^{\diamond}_{\nu}=f^{\diamond}_{\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu})}
,
z^{+}_{\nu}=z^{+}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu}))
and
z^{\diamond}_{\nu}=z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu}))
. We have the following asymptotic expansions at
(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})^{-}
:

\displaystyle f^{\bullet}_{\nu}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{z^{+}_{\nu}}-\frac{1}{z^{+}_{\nu}}\left(\frac{1}{z^{+}_{\nu}}-\sqrt{z^{+}_{\nu}}\partial_{z_{1}}f^{\diamond}_{\nu}(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})\right)\left(z^{+}_{\nu}-z_{1}\right)-\partial_{z_{1}}f^{\diamond}_{\nu}(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})\left(z^{\diamond}_{\nu}-z_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\aleph^{\bullet}(\nu)\left((z^{\diamond}_{\nu}-z_{2})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{z^{+}_{\nu}}}(z^{+}_{\nu}-z_{1})\right)^{a(\nu)-1/2}+\mathcal{O}\left((z^{\diamond}_{\nu}-z_{2})^{2}+(z^{+}_{\nu}-z_{1})^{2}\right),

\displaystyle f^{\diamond}_{\nu}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=z^{\diamond}_{\nu}-\partial_{z_{1}}f^{\diamond}_{\nu}(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})\left(z^{+}_{\nu}-z_{1}\right)-\left(1-\sqrt{z^{+}_{\nu}}\,\partial_{z_{1}}f^{\diamond}_{\nu}(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})\right)\left(z^{\diamond}_{\nu}-z_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\aleph^{\diamond}(\nu)\left((z^{\diamond}_{\nu}-z_{2})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{z^{+}_{\nu}}}(z^{+}_{\nu}-z_{1})\right)^{a(\nu)-1/2}+\mathcal{O}\left((z^{\diamond}_{\nu}-z_{2})^{2}+(z^{+}_{\nu}-z_{1})^{2}\right),

where
\partial_{z_{1}}f^{\diamond}_{\nu}(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})\in(0,1)
,
\aleph^{\diamond}(\nu),\aleph^{\bullet}(\nu)>0
are explicit functions of
\nu
and where we recall that

a(\nu)=\begin{cases}5/3&\text{for $\nu<\nu_{c}$,}\\
7/3&\text{for $\nu=\nu_{c}$.}\end{cases}

(4.49)

Remark 4.18
.

Our proof of Lemma
4.17
can also be used to show that the functions
f^{\bullet}_{\nu}
and
f^{\diamond}_{\nu}
are analytic in a neighborhood of
(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})
when
\nu>\nu_{c}
. We do not include this part here as we do not use that fact, and also because it is already a consequence (see for example Miermont
[
60
]
) of the fact that the weight sequence
\mathbf{q}(\nu,t_{\nu})
is regular critical when
\nu>\nu_{c}
since the root face of the associated random map has exponential tails (see Proposition
5.1
).

Proof of Lemma
4.17
.

Fix
\nu\in(0,\nu_{c}]
and
(z_{1},z_{2})\in(0,z^{+}_{\nu}]\times(0,z^{\diamond}_{\nu}]
.
We start with the expressions for
f^{\bullet}
and
f^{\diamond}
given in Proposition
4.16
. Recall the notation
a_{+}=1-\sqrt{\nu t_{\nu}^{3}}(z_{2}+2\sqrt{z_{1}})
and
a_{-}=1-\sqrt{\nu t_{\nu}^{3}}(z_{2}-2\sqrt{z_{1}})
.
The change of variable

z=\phi(z_{1},z_{2};\xi):=\frac{1}{a_{-}}+\left(\frac{1}{a_{+}}-\frac{1}{a_{-}}\right)\xi

in the expressions of
f_{\nu}^{\bullet}
and
f_{\nu}^{\diamond}
give:

\displaystyle f_{\nu}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=(\nu t_{\nu})^{3/2}\,2z_{2}+\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{a_{+}a_{-}}}\,\int_{0}^{1}\,\frac{1-\sqrt{\nu t^{3}_{\nu}}-\frac{1}{\phi(z_{1},z_{2};\xi)}}{2z_{1}}\,\xi^{-1/2}(1-\xi)^{-1/2}Q^{+}\left(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}\phi(z_{1},z_{2};\xi)\right)d\xi,

\displaystyle f_{\nu}^{\diamond}

\displaystyle(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=(\nu t_{\nu})^{3/2}(2z_{1}+z_{2}^{2})+\frac{\sqrt{\nu t_{\nu}^{3}}}{\pi\sqrt{a_{+}a_{-}}}\,\int_{0}^{1}\,\xi^{-1/2}(1-\xi)^{-1/2}Q^{+}\left(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}\phi(z_{1},z_{2};\xi)\right)d\xi.

Note that for
(z_{1},z_{2})\in(0,z_{\nu}^{+}]\times(0,z_{\nu}^{\diamond})
and
\xi\in[0,1]
, we have:

\phi(z_{1},z_{2};\xi)\in\left[\frac{1}{a_{-}},\frac{1}{a_{+}}\right]\subset\left[\frac{1}{1+2\sqrt{\nu t_{\nu}^{3}}z^{+}_{\nu}},\frac{1}{1-1/y_{+}(\nu,t_{\nu})}\right]\subset(0,2],

(4.50)

and
\phi(z_{1},z_{2};\xi)=2
, if and only if
(z_{1},z_{2},\xi)=(z_{\nu}^{+},z_{\nu}^{\diamond},1)
.
Moreover recall that, since
\nu\leqslant\nu_{c}
, the dominant singularity
y_{+}(\nu,t_{\nu})
of
y\mapsto Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)
is equal to 2.

The general strategy to compute the singular expansion of these integrals is then the following.
We are first going to prove that, if we remove from
Q^{+}
its first singular terms in its development at
y=2
, the first terms of the asymptotic development of the integral are linear and quadratic terms.
Then, we prove that the singular part in the development is driven by the singular part in the asymptotic behavior of
y\mapsto Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)
around 2 and calculate it.

We start with
f^{\diamond}
as the calculations are slightly simpler in this case. From Proposition
3.10
, we know that
y\mapsto Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)
is analytic for
|y|<2
and we can compute the following expansion as
y\to 2^{-}
:

\begin{split}Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)=&Q^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},2t_{\nu})\\
&+\aleph_{2/3}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\cdot(2-y)^{2/3}+\aleph_{1}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\cdot(2-y)+\aleph_{4/3}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\cdot(2-y)^{4/3}\\
&+\aleph_{5/3}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\cdot(2-y)^{5/3}+\aleph_{2}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\cdot(2-y)^{2}+o\left((2-y)^{2}\right)\end{split}

(4.51)

where we recall that the coefficients
\aleph_{i}^{Q^{+}}
are explicit functions of
\nu
such that
\aleph_{2/3}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)=0
if and only if
\nu=\nu_{c}
and
\aleph_{4/3}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\neq 0
for
\nu\leqslant\nu_{c}
.

Set

\varphi(y)=Q^{+}(t_{\nu},t_{\nu}y)-\aleph_{2/3}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\cdot(2-y)^{2/3}-\aleph_{4/3}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\cdot(2-y)^{4/3}-\aleph_{5/3}^{Q^{+}}(\nu)\cdot(2-y)^{5/3}.

In view of (
4.50
) and (
4.51
), the function
\varphi
is twice differentiable on
(0,2)
and we have:

\int_{0}^{1}\xi^{-1/2}(1-\xi)^{-1/2}\left|\varphi^{\prime}\left(\phi(z_{1},z_{2};\xi)\right)\right|d\xi<\infty

and

\int_{0}^{1}\xi^{-1/2}(1-\xi)^{-1/2}\left|\varphi^{\prime\prime}\left(\phi(z_{1},z_{2};\xi)\right)\right|d\xi<\infty

Together with the fact that
\frac{1}{a_{-}}
and
\frac{1}{a_{+}}
are twice differentiable on
[0,z^{+}_{\nu}]\times[0,z^{\diamond}_{\nu}]
, this implies that the function
I_{\varphi}
defined by:

I_{\varphi}(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\,\xi^{-1/2}(1-\xi)^{-1/2}\varphi\left(\frac{1}{a_{-}}+\left(\frac{1}{a_{+}}-\frac{1}{a_{-}}\right)\xi\right)d\xi

is twice differentiable on
[0,z^{+}_{\nu}]\times[0,z^{\diamond}_{\nu}]
. Therefore, as
z_{1}\to(z^{+}_{\nu})^{-}
and
z_{2}\to(z^{\diamond}_{\nu})^{-}
we get the non singular expansion:

I_{\varphi}(z_{1},z_{2})=I_{\varphi}(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})+\nabla I_{\varphi}(z^{+}_{\nu},z^{\diamond}_{\nu})\cdot(z_{1}-z^{+}_{\nu},z_{2}-z^{\diamond}_{\nu})+\mathcal{O}\left((z_{1}-z^{+}_{\nu})^{2}+(z_{2}-z^{\diamond}_{\nu})^{2}\right).

We now move to the singular part in the development of the function
f^{\diamond}_{\nu}
. It comes from the terms of the form
(2-y)^{p}
with
p=2/3,4/3
and
5/3
that we subtracted. Indeed, for these values of
p
, their contribution to the integral is equal to:

\displaystyle I_{p}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\,\xi^{-1/2}(1-\xi)^{-1/2}\left(2-\left(\frac{1}{a_{-}}+\left(\frac{1}{a_{+}}-\frac{1}{a_{-}}\right)\xi\right)\right)^{p}d\xi,

\displaystyle=\left({2-\frac{1}{a_{-}}}\right)^{p}\,\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\,\xi^{-1/2}(1-\xi)^{-1/2}\left(1-\left(\frac{\frac{1}{a_{+}}-\frac{1}{a_{-}}}{{2-\frac{1}{a_{-}}}}\right)\xi\right)^{p}d\xi.

To compute this integral, we rewrite it as the Euler integral representation of a hypergeometric function. Indeed, when
z_{1}\to(z^{+}_{\nu})^{-}
and
z_{2}\to(z^{\diamond}_{\nu})^{-}
, we have
\frac{1}{a_{+}}\to 2^{-}
and
\frac{\frac{1}{a_{+}}-\frac{1}{a_{-}}}{{2-\frac{1}{a_{-}}}}\to 1^{-}
, so that:

\displaystyle I_{p}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\left({2-\frac{1}{a_{-}}}\right)^{p}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(-p,\frac{1}{2};1;\frac{\frac{1}{a_{+}}-\frac{1}{a_{-}}}{{2-\frac{1}{a_{-}}}}\right).

This allows us to use the singular behavior at
1^{-}
of
\,{}_{2}F_{1}
, which is of the form:

_{2}F_{1}\left(-p,\frac{1}{2};1;u\right)=a_{p}+b_{p}\,(1-u)+c_{p}\left(1-u\right)^{p+1/2}+\mathcal{O}\left(1-u\right)^{2}

(4.52)

as
u\to 1^{-}
, where
a_{p}
,
b_{p}
and
c_{p}
are explicit (see the Maple file
[
1
]
).

We have
