\partial\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}^{\nu}(n)
are microscopic and their size divided by
n
vanish in probability as
n\to\infty
. This implies that we have the following convergence in distribution for the Gromov–Hausdorff topology:

\frac{1}{n}\partial\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}^{\nu}(n)\underset{n\to\infty}{\rightarrow}\left(1+\frac{\aleph_{1}^{Z^{+}}(\nu)}{1+Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}x_{\nu})}\right)\mathscr{C}_{1}.

The constant in the right hand side of the previous equation is explicit and calculated in the Maple companion file
[
1
]
.

Case
\nu<\nu_{c}
.

In this case, the reproduction law
\mu_{\nu}
is critical and we have from (
7.2
) and Lemma
3.16
:

\mu_{\nu}(k)\underset{k\to\infty}{\sim}\frac{3}{4\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{-\aleph^{Z^{+}}(\nu)}{1+Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}x_{\nu})}\,k^{-5/2}.

From there classical results (see for instance
[
33
]
and
[
47
]
) imply that
n^{-1/3}\mathrm{JS}(\mathrm{Tree}_{2}(\partial\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}^{\nu}(n)))
converges in distribution toward the
3/2
-stable Lévy tree for the Gromov-Hausdorff topology. The canonical looptree
\mathrm{Loop}(\mathrm{JS}(\mathrm{Tree}_{2}(\partial\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}^{\nu}(n)))
associated to
\mathrm{JS}(\mathrm{Tree}_{2}(\partial\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}^{\nu}(n)))
as defined in the introduction and in
[
30
]
, illustrated in Figure
9
in the present setting, has a Gromov–Hausdorff distance to
\partial\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}^{\nu}(n))
bounded by twice its height. The results of
[
30
, Section 4.2]
show that we have the following convergence in distribution for the Gromov–Hausdorff topology:

\frac{1}{n^{2/3}}\partial\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}^{\nu}(n))\underset{n\to\infty}{\rightarrow}\left(\frac{-\aleph^{Z^{+}}(\nu)}{1+Z^{+}(\nu,t_{\nu},t_{\nu}x_{\nu})}\right)^{-2/3}\,\mathscr{L}_{3/2},

which finishes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Generating series of
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann maps of the cylinder

A.1
Parametrization in terms of Zhukovsky’s variables

In the book by Eynard
[
34
]
, the generating series
W_{\mathbf{q},2}
is denoted
W_{2}^{(0)}
where the “2” stands for the number of boundaries and the “(0)” for the genus of the underlying surface (which is equal to 0 here, since we only consider maps on the sphere). In Theorem 3.2.1, Eynard obtains the following rational parametrization for
W_{\mathbf{q},2}
in terms of the so-called Zhukovsky’s variables:

Theorem A.1

(Theorem 3.2.1 of
[
34
]
)
.

W_{\mathbf{q},2}\Big(z(x_{1}),z(x_{2})\Big)z^{\prime}(x_{1})z^{\prime}(x_{2})=\frac{-1}{(x_{1}x_{2}-1)^{2}},

(A.1)

where
z(x)=z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q})+\sqrt{z^{+}(\mathbf{q})}\Big(x+\dfrac{1}{x}\Big)
.

In
[
34
]
, the rational parametrization is in fact only given with the additional assumption that the faces have bounded degree. We refer to
[
15
, Section 6]
for a combinatorial derivation of the rational parametrization, which does not rely on this assumption.

We can then check from Theorem
A.1
that Proposition
4.6
is correct. It suffices to replace
W_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}(z)
in (
4.26
) by its following expression in terms of the rational parametrization, see
[
34
, Section 3.1.3]
:

W_{\mathbf{q},\bullet}\Big(z(x)\Big)=\sqrt{z^{+}(\mathbf{q})}\left(x-\frac{1}{x}\right),

and to check that one gets the same expression as in (
A.1
). We perform these computations in the Maple companion file
[
1
]
.

A.2
Derivation of Proposition
4.6
via slice decomposition

The slice decomposition, which has been introduced by Bouttier and Guitter in
[
22
]
, consists in cutting a planar map along some well-chosen geodesics. The submaps obtained after the cutting, which have geodesic boundaries by construction, are called
slices
.

Originally designed to deal with irreducible maps, the slice decomposition can in fact be applied to general maps, see
[
21
, Chapter 2]
. In this presentation, Bouttier focuses on bipartite maps, but explains in Remark 2.1 how to extend his results to non-bipartite maps. All the bijective constructions remain valid in the non-bipartite case, the only modifications and complications appear in the enumerative part.

To recall the main bijective results obtained via the slice decompositions, we start with a few (classical) definitions. A
Motzkin path
is a lattice path made of steps
(0,-1)
,
(0,0)
and
(0,1)
, which starts at
(0,0)
. We consider in particular Motzkin bridges, which are paths ending on the
x
-axis and Motzkin excursions, which are paths ending at height
-1
and which stay non-negative before their very last step.

For the purpose of map enumeration, we consider the enumeration of
weighted Motzkin paths
, where each step carries the following weight:

•

each
down-step

(-1,0)
carries a weight
1
,

•

each
level-step

(0,0)
carries a weight
z^{\diamond}(\mathbf{q})
,

•

each
up-step

(1,0)
carries a weight
z^{+}(\mathbf{q})
,

and the weight of a path is the product of the weights of its steps. For
h\in\mathbb{Z}
, we let
M_{h}(z)
to be the generating series of weighted Motzkin walks that end at height
h
, and similarly
E(z)
denotes the generating series of weighted Motzkin excursions, that is:

M_{h}(z)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\{\text{Motzkin walks}\\
\text{ending at height }h\}\end{subarray}}\frac{\text{weight}(w)}{z^{\text{length of }w}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad E(z)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\{\text{Motzkin}\\
\text{excursions}\}\end{subarray}}\frac{\text{weight}(w)}{z^{\text{length of }w}}.

Then, it follows directly from the discussion following Theorem 2.1 in
[
21
]
, that:

Theorem A.2
.

We have the following equality of weighted generating series:

W_{\mathbf{q},2}(z_{1},z_{2})=\sum_{h\geqslant 1}hM_{h}(z_{1})M_{-h}(z_{2}).

(A.2)

The expression for
W_{\mathbf{q},2}(z_{1},z_{2})
given in Proposition
4.6
then follows easily from this result and classical enumeration of Motzkin paths.

Appendix B
Analytic combinatorics toolbox

We gather in this appendix two results from the book of Flajolet and Sedgewick
[
36
]
that are used several times in the present work.

B.1
Transfer theorem for algebraic series

In this work, all our generating series are algebraic and fall into the framework of transfer theorems developed by Flajolet and Sedgewick.

Theorem B.1

(Corollary VI.1 of
[
36
]
)
.

Suppose that
f(z)
is algebraic and has a unique dominant singularity at
\rho>0
where it satisfies

f(z)\underset{z\to\rho}{\sim}c\,(1-\rho)^{-a}

for some
c\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
a\notin\{0,-1,-2.\ldots\}
. Then the coefficients of
f
satisfy

[z^{n}]f(z)\underset{n\to\infty}{\sim}c\,\frac{n^{a-1}}{\Gamma(a)},

where
\Gamma
denotes Euler’s Gamma function.

Proof.

The only thing that one has to verify in order to apply Corollary VI.1 of
[
36
]
is the
\Delta
-analyticity of
f
. In our case,
f
is algebraic, so it has finitely many singularities.
Since
\rho
is the unique dominant singularity, this implies that

f
is analytic in a slit open disk
D(0,\rho+\varepsilon)\setminus[\rho,\rho+\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
and the transfer theorem follows.
∎

B.2
Hadamard product of series

Let
f(z)=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}a_{n}z^{n}
and
g(z)=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}b_{n}z^{n}
be two formal power series. The Hadamard product of
f
and
g
is the formal power series defined by

f\odot g(z)=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}a_{n}b_{n}z^{n}.

When the functions
f
and
g
are analytic in a large enough domain, the Hadamard product has a representation as a contour integral:

Theorem B.2

(Equation (58) in
[
36
]
)
.

Let
f
and
g
be two functions which are analytic in the same domain
\Delta\subset\mathbb{C}
. Then

f\odot g(z)=\oint_{\gamma}f(w)g\left(\frac{w}{z}\right)\frac{dw}{w},

where the contour
\gamma
in the
w
-plane is such that
f(w)
and
g(z/w)
are analytic. In other words,
\gamma\subset\Delta\cap(z\Delta^{-1})
.
